Radio is associated with sales, so newspaper can appear positively related to sales in a simple regression even when its coefficient is close to zero after adjusting for TV and radio in a multiple model. Newspaper acts as a surrogate for radio's association. This illustrates why the individual role of a predictor should be assessed in the context of relevant other predictors rather than by relying only on separate regressions.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the newspaper-radio-sales example and explain how correlation between predictors changes the interpretation of a simple regression coefficient.
Key point:
- Omitted correlated predictors can distort simple-regression interpretations.
- Multiple regression estimates each predictor's association conditional on the others.
18. Q: What is the overall F-test in multiple linear regression?
A: Show answer
The overall F-test evaluates the null hypothesis that all slope coefficients in the multiple regression model are zero against the alternative that at least one is nonzero. A large F-statistic relative to what would be expected under the null provides evidence that the predictors, taken together, are associated with the response. The test answers whether the model has any explanatory association beyond the intercept-only model; it does not establish that every predictor is useful. In the Advertising example, the large F-statistic provides strong evidence that at least one advertising medium is related to sales. Individual coefficient tests are needed to investigate specific predictors after adjustment for the others.
Study instruction:
- State the null and alternative hypotheses for the overall F-test and explain what a significant result does and does not establish.
Key point:
- The overall F-test evaluates the predictors jointly.
- A significant result means at least one slope is nonzero under the model assumptions.
19. Q: How do individual t-tests differ from the overall F-test in multiple regression?
A: Show answer
An individual t-test evaluates whether one coefficient is zero after adjusting for the other predictors in the model. The overall F-test evaluates whether all slope coefficients are zero jointly. Individual t-tests help identify predictors with evidence of a partial association, while the F-test assesses whether the model as a whole contributes explanatory information. For a single coefficient, the square of its t-statistic equals the corresponding one-variable partial F-statistic. The overall test is especially useful when many predictors are present because examining many individual p-values can produce false discoveries even when no true associations exist.
Study instruction:
- Compare the hypotheses tested by an individual coefficient t-test and the overall model F-test in the same fitted model.
Key point:
- Individual t-tests examine one coefficient conditionally.
- The overall F-test examines the full set of slopes jointly.
20. Q: Why can examining many individual p-values create false discoveries?
A: Show answer
When many hypotheses are tested, some small p-values can occur by chance even if none of the predictors is truly associated with the response. The chapter illustrates this with 100 predictors: if all null hypotheses are true, approximately five p-values may fall below 0.05 simply by chance. The probability of observing at least one apparently significant result becomes high when many tests are conducted. The overall F-test addresses the joint question of whether any predictor is associated with the response and adjusts for the number of predictors in the model. Individual p-values remain useful, but should be interpreted in light of multiplicity, model selection, and the study's goals.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the expected number of p-values below 0.05 when 100 independent null hypotheses are tested and explain why this does not automatically indicate real effects.
Key point:
- Multiple testing increases the chance of false-positive findings.
- Joint tests and careful variable-selection procedures help control overinterpretation.
21. Q: Why does ordinary least squares become problematic when the number of predictors exceeds the number of observations?
A: Show answer
When p is greater than n, there are more slope coefficients to estimate than observations available to identify them. In this high-dimensional setting, the ordinary least squares multiple regression solution is not uniquely determined in the usual way, and the standard F-test and many familiar inferential procedures cannot be applied as described. The chapter points forward to variable-selection and regularization methods for such settings. Analysts should recognize the dimensionality problem before fitting a model and avoid treating software output as reliable merely because a computational routine returns a result. The ratio of observations to predictors is an important constraint on model complexity and coefficient stability.
Study instruction:
- Given n and p, determine whether p exceeds n and explain why a conventional least squares model may not be estimable uniquely.
Key point:
- More predictors than observations creates a high-dimensional estimation problem.
- Alternative methods are needed when ordinary least squares cannot identify all coefficients.
22. Q: What is variable selection in multiple regression?
A: Show answer
Variable selection is the process of deciding which predictors should be included in a model. It is useful because many applications contain predictors that contribute little information once other variables are considered. A smaller model may be easier to interpret and may avoid unnecessary complexity. The chapter discusses evaluating subsets of predictors with criteria such as Mallows' Cp, AIC, BIC, adjusted R-squared, and residual plots. The goal is not simply to remove every variable with a large p-value; model selection should consider fit, predictive performance, interpretability, and the assumptions of the analysis. The selected model should be justified by the intended use and the available data.
Study instruction:
- Choose a candidate set of predictors and explain how at least two model-selection criteria could be used to compare subsets.
Key point:
- Variable selection seeks a useful subset of predictors.
- Fit, complexity, and interpretability should be considered together.
23. Q: Why is exhaustive best-subset selection often infeasible?
A: Show answer
With p candidate predictors, there are 2^p possible subsets, including the model with no predictors and the model with all predictors. The number grows exponentially. For 30 predictors, there are more than one billion subsets, making it impractical to fit and compare every model in many settings. The chapter therefore introduces forward, backward, and mixed selection as more computationally efficient alternatives. These procedures do not guarantee the globally best subset because they search only a restricted sequence of models, but they can make variable selection feasible when p is too large for exhaustive search.
Study instruction:
- Calculate the number of possible subsets for p = 5, p = 10, and p = 30, then explain why the search becomes expensive quickly.
Key point:
- Best-subset search grows exponentially with predictor count.
- Stepwise procedures reduce the search burden but may miss the best overall subset.
24. Q: How does forward selection work?
A: Show answer
Forward selection begins with a null model containing an intercept but no predictors. It fits candidate one-predictor models and adds the variable that gives the best improvement under the chosen criterion. It then considers adding another variable to the current model and continues one variable at a time until a stopping rule is reached. Because the procedure is greedy, an early choice may later become redundant or prevent the algorithm from finding a better combination. Forward selection can be used when p is greater than n because it does not begin by fitting the full model, although the chosen stopping rule and evaluation method remain important.
Study instruction:
- Trace forward selection for three predictors, showing the null model, first addition, second addition, and a possible stopping condition.
Key point:
- Forward selection builds a model by adding predictors sequentially.
- Early choices can constrain later choices.
25. Q: How does backward selection work, and what limits its use?
A: Show answer
Backward selection begins with all candidate predictors in the model and removes the least statistically significant variable at each step, often the one with the largest p-value. The process continues until a stopping rule is met, such as all remaining predictors having p-values below a threshold. Because it requires fitting the full model initially, ordinary backward selection cannot be used when p exceeds n in the setting described by the chapter. The procedure can also be sensitive to the order of removal and the chosen significance threshold. Its results should be treated as one model-search strategy rather than an automatic proof that retained variables are the only important ones.
Study instruction:
- Apply backward selection to a hypothetical model and identify why the procedure cannot start when the full model is not estimable.
Key point:
- Backward selection removes predictors from a full model.
- It requires the full model to be fit at the outset.
26. Q: What is mixed or stepwise selection?
A: Show answer
Mixed selection combines forward and backward steps. It begins with no predictors, adds variables that improve the model, and can remove previously included variables if their evidence weakens after new predictors enter. This matters because a predictor's p-value can change when other correlated predictors are added. The procedure continues until the variables in the model meet the inclusion criteria and variables outside the model would not qualify for addition. Mixed selection can correct some limitations of pure forward selection, but it remains a data-driven search and can produce unstable results when predictors are highly correlated or the sample is small.
Study instruction:
- Create a short sequence in which a variable is first added and later removed after another predictor enters the model.
Key point:
- Mixed selection alternates between adding and removing predictors.
- Coefficient evidence can change as the model changes.
27. Q: Why does R-squared always increase or stay the same when predictors are added to an ordinary least squares model?
A: Show answer
Adding a predictor expands the set of models the least squares procedure can fit. The enlarged model can always reproduce the smaller model by assigning the new coefficient a value of zero, so its minimum training RSS cannot be larger. Since R-squared is one minus RSS divided by the fixed total sum of squares, R-squared cannot decrease when another predictor is added. This does not mean the added variable improves prediction on new data or contributes a meaningful relationship. The increase can be negligible and may simply reflect extra flexibility. Adjusted R-squared and other criteria penalize unnecessary complexity more directly.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the model with an added predictor can reproduce the original fit and connect that fact to the behavior of R-squared.
Key point:
- Training R-squared is non-decreasing as predictors are added.
- Its increase alone is not sufficient evidence that a variable is useful.
28. Q: Why can residual standard error increase when a predictor is added even though RSS cannot increase?
A: Show answer
RSE includes a degrees-of-freedom adjustment, not just RSS. When another predictor is added, RSS cannot increase, but the denominator used to calculate RSE also decreases because more coefficients have been estimated. If the reduction in RSS is too small to offset the loss of degrees of freedom, RSE can increase. The chapter uses the Advertising example to show that adding newspaper spending to a model already containing TV and radio barely changes fit and can slightly increase RSE. This is why analysts should consider multiple measures of model quality rather than assuming every additional predictor improves every fit statistic.
Study instruction:
- Compare two nested models with similar RSS values and explain how the degrees-of-freedom adjustment can change RSE.
Key point:
- RSE depends on both RSS and the number of estimated parameters.
- Adding a weak predictor can increase RSE.
29. Q: Why should regression residual plots be examined in addition to RSE and R-squared?
A: Show answer
RSE and R-squared summarize fit numerically but can conceal systematic patterns in the residuals. A residual plot can reveal curvature, changing variance, clusters, or other structure that suggests the model assumptions are inadequate. In the Advertising example, residual patterns indicate that sales may be higher when TV and radio budgets are combined than an additive linear model predicts. A model can have a high R-squared and still omit an important interaction or non-linear pattern. Graphical diagnostics therefore complement numerical measures and help identify what kind of model extension may be needed.
Study instruction:
- Draw or inspect a residual plot and distinguish a random cloud from a systematic curve or funnel pattern.
Key point:
- Numerical fit measures do not reveal every model deficiency.
- Residual plots help diagnose omitted structure and assumption violations.
30. Q: What are the three sources of uncertainty in a multiple-regression prediction described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
First, the estimated coefficients differ from the unknown population coefficients, so the fitted regression surface is uncertain. Second, the assumed linear form may be only an approximation to the true relationship, creating model bias. Third, even if the true function were known, an individual response would still vary because of the random error term. The first source is estimation uncertainty, the second is uncertainty from model form, and the third is irreducible response variation. A prediction for an individual case must account for more uncertainty than an estimate of the average response at the same predictor values.
Study instruction:
- Classify each source of uncertainty as coefficient estimation, model-form error, or irreducible individual variation.
Key point:
- Predictions involve several distinct sources of uncertainty.
- The individual response includes irreducible error beyond uncertainty in the mean relationship.
31. Q: What is the difference between a confidence interval and a prediction interval in regression?
A: Show answer
A confidence interval quantifies uncertainty about the average response at a specified predictor value. A prediction interval quantifies uncertainty about an individual future response at that same predictor value. The prediction interval is wider because it includes both uncertainty in the estimated mean function and the additional variation of an individual observation around that mean. In the Advertising example, the 95 percent prediction interval for a particular market is much wider than the confidence interval for average sales across many comparable markets. The two intervals answer different questions and should not be substituted for one another in a report or decision.
Study instruction:
- For a specified predictor combination, state whether the decision concerns an average outcome or an individual outcome and choose the appropriate interval.
Key point:
- Confidence intervals describe uncertainty in the mean response.
- Prediction intervals describe uncertainty in an individual response and are wider.
32. Q: How are qualitative predictors included in a linear regression model?
A: Show answer
A qualitative predictor is represented through indicator or dummy variables. For a predictor with two levels, one can create a zero-one variable that identifies one of the levels. The intercept then represents the expected response for the reference group, and the dummy-variable coefficient represents the difference between the two group means in the simple model. The chapter's Credit example uses gender to illustrate this coding. The numerical coding is a representation of categories, not a claim that the categories form a measured scale. Coefficients should be interpreted in terms of group contrasts and the chosen reference category.
Study instruction:
- Create a zero-one indicator for a two-level factor and interpret the intercept and dummy coefficient in the context of the response.
Key point:
- Dummy variables allow categorical predictors to enter a linear model.
- Coefficients represent contrasts relative to a reference group.
33. Q: How does changing the reference coding of a binary predictor affect a regression model?
A: Show answer
Reversing which group is coded zero and which is coded one changes the numerical interpretation of the intercept and slope coefficient, but it does not change the fitted group predictions. With one coding, the intercept represents the average response for the first group and the slope is the difference for the second group relative to the first. With reversed coding, the intercept represents the other group and the slope changes sign. The fitted values remain identical. Coding should therefore be chosen for interpretability, and the reference group should be stated clearly when reporting coefficients.
Study instruction:
- Recode a two-level variable using the opposite reference group and verify that the predicted values are unchanged even though the coefficients differ.
Key point:
- Reference coding changes coefficient interpretation, not the model's fitted group values.
- Always identify the baseline category.
34. Q: How are qualitative predictors with more than two levels represented in linear regression?
A: Show answer
A factor with K levels is represented using K minus 1 dummy variables, with one category serving as the baseline. Each coefficient describes the difference between its category and the baseline, holding other predictors fixed. The chapter's ethnicity example uses two dummy variables for three categories and interprets the intercept as the baseline group's mean response. The chosen baseline is arbitrary, and changing it changes the coefficient values and their interpretations but not the fitted predictions for the categories. Omitting one dummy variable avoids perfect linear dependence among the indicators and provides a clear reference for the contrasts.
Study instruction:
- For a four-level factor, identify the number of dummy variables required and state how each coefficient would be interpreted relative to the baseline.
Key point:
- A K-level factor uses K minus 1 dummy variables in the reference coding approach.
- The omitted category is the baseline for interpreting contrasts.
35. Q: What is the additive assumption in standard multiple linear regression?
A: Show answer
The additive assumption means that the effect of changing one predictor on the response does not depend on the values of the other predictors. In a model with TV and radio spending, the additive form assigns a fixed slope to TV regardless of the radio budget. This makes the model straightforward to interpret, but it may be unrealistic if the predictors reinforce or weaken each other's effects. When the effect of one predictor depends on another, an interaction term can be added. Residual patterns and substantive knowledge can help identify situations where the additive assumption is questionable.
Study instruction:
- Explain what it means for the TV effect to be constant at all radio spending levels and identify a business situation where that assumption may fail.
Key point:
- Additivity assumes predictor effects do not depend on other predictors.
- Interaction terms allow conditional effects to vary.
36. Q: What is an interaction effect in linear regression?
A: Show answer
An interaction occurs when the effect of one predictor on the response depends on the value of another predictor. The chapter's Advertising example suggests that combining TV and radio spending can produce more sales than would be predicted by simply adding their separate effects. An interaction term is formed by multiplying the two predictors and including that product in the model. The coefficient of the product term describes how the slope for one predictor changes as the other predictor changes. Interactions can represent synergy, diminishing effects, or other forms of conditional association, depending on the estimated coefficient and variable scales.
Study instruction:
- Identify two predictors that could plausibly interact and explain why the effect of one may vary with the level of the other.
Key point:
- Interactions model dependencies between predictor effects.
- A product term allows one slope to change with another predictor.
37. Q: How should the coefficient of a quantitative-by-quantitative interaction be interpreted?
A: Show answer
In a model with X1, X2, and their product X1X2, the marginal effect of X1 is no longer a constant coefficient. It is the coefficient for X1 plus the interaction coefficient multiplied by X2. As X2 changes, the effect associated with a one-unit increase in X1 changes. The chapter illustrates this with production lines and workers: the benefit of adding a production line depends on how many workers are available to operate it. In the Advertising example, the interaction coefficient measures how the effectiveness of TV spending changes as radio spending increases, or vice versa. Interpretation should use the full conditional expression, not the interaction coefficient in isolation.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a two-predictor interaction model to show the conditional slope of X1 at a given value of X2.
Key point:
- The interaction coefficient modifies the effect of one predictor as the other changes.
- Main effects and interaction terms must be interpreted together.
38. Q: How can an interaction between a quantitative and a qualitative predictor be interpreted?
A: Show answer
An interaction between a numeric predictor and a group indicator allows the slope of the numeric predictor to differ between groups. Without the interaction, the model may give the groups different intercepts but parallel regression lines. With the interaction, both intercepts and slopes can differ. The chapter uses income and student status to show that the relationship between income and credit-card balance may not be the same for students and non-students. The interaction coefficient measures the difference in slopes between the groups. This can be important when the same change in a quantitative predictor has different implications across categories.
Study instruction:
- Draw parallel lines for a model without interaction and non-parallel lines for a model with interaction, then explain the slope difference.
Key point:
- A qualitative-by-quantitative interaction permits group-specific slopes.
- Group effects may differ in both intercept and slope.
39. Q: What is the hierarchical principle for models containing interactions?
A: Show answer
The hierarchical principle recommends retaining the main effects of predictors when their interaction is included, even if the individual main-effect p-values are not small. An interaction term is constructed from the predictors and its meaning depends on their presence in the model. Removing a main effect can change the interpretation of the interaction and can create a model specification that is difficult to justify. The principle is a modeling convention that supports coherent interpretation, not a claim that every main-effect coefficient must be statistically significant. Analysts should report the hierarchy used and explain the conditional nature of the fitted effects.
Study instruction:
- Given a model with X1, X2, and X1X2, explain why retaining both main effects supports interpretation of the interaction.
Key point:
- Interaction models generally retain their component main effects.
- Hierarchy supports coherent specification and interpretation.
40. Q: How can polynomial regression represent a non-linear relationship while remaining a linear regression model?
A: Show answer
Polynomial regression adds transformed versions of a predictor, such as X squared or X cubed, as additional predictors. The response can then vary along a curved function of the original X, even though the model remains linear in its unknown coefficients. For example, a quadratic model uses X and X squared, allowing a curved relationship between horsepower and fuel economy. Standard least squares software can estimate the coefficients because the transformed terms are treated as predictors. The analyst should choose polynomial degree carefully: adding many powers can create an unnecessarily wiggly fit and may overfit the data.
Study instruction:
- Write a quadratic regression equation and identify why it is linear in its coefficients even though its fitted curve is not a straight line in X.
Key point:
- Polynomial terms extend the shape of a linear model.
- The model remains linear in the coefficients, not necessarily in the original predictor.
41. Q: How should an analyst use residual plots to detect non-linearity?
A: Show answer
In simple regression, residuals can be plotted against the predictor. In multiple regression, residuals are often plotted against fitted values because there are several predictors. If the linear model is adequate, the residuals should show no systematic pattern around zero. A curved or U-shaped pattern suggests that the model has failed to capture a non-linear relationship. The chapter's Auto example shows a curved residual pattern for a linear model of fuel economy on horsepower; adding a quadratic horsepower term reduces the pattern. Residual plots are diagnostic evidence and should be interpreted alongside subject knowledge and other model checks.
Study instruction:
- Inspect residuals versus fitted values for a model and describe whether the points form a random cloud or a systematic curve.
Key point:
- Structured residual patterns can indicate non-linearity.
- Transformations or more flexible methods may be needed when curvature remains.
42. Q: Why is correlation among error terms a problem for linear regression inference?
A: Show answer
The usual standard-error formulas assume that errors from different observations are uncorrelated. If errors are correlated, the reported standard errors can underestimate the true uncertainty. Confidence intervals may then be too narrow and p-values too small, leading analysts to overstate the evidence for predictor effects. Correlation can occur in time-series data when adjacent observations share common conditions, but it can also occur among related individuals or observations exposed to shared environments. The chapter emphasizes good experimental design and residual plots over time as ways to identify and reduce this risk. The independence structure of the data should be understood before relying on standard inferential output.
Study instruction:
- Describe a data collection design that could produce correlated errors and explain how it would affect confidence intervals and p-values.
Key point:
- Correlated errors undermine standard error calculations.
- Dependence among observations must be considered in design and analysis.
43. Q: How can residual plots reveal correlation among errors in time-series data?
A: Show answer
When residuals are plotted in time order, uncorrelated errors should not display a systematic sequence. Positive correlation can produce tracking, where adjacent residuals tend to have similar signs or magnitudes. A pattern of long runs above or below zero suggests that the model has not captured temporal structure. If observations are duplicated or otherwise dependent, treating them as independent can make the apparent sample size larger than the true amount of independent information. Analysts should therefore preserve time order and investigate dependence before interpreting ordinary regression standard errors and tests.
Study instruction:
- Plot residuals in chronological order and identify evidence of tracking or repeated runs that may indicate dependence.
Key point:
- Time-ordered residual patterns can reveal correlated errors.
- Apparent sample size may overstate independent information when observations are dependent.
44. Q: What is non-constant error variance, or heteroscedasticity?
A: Show answer
Heteroscedasticity occurs when the variance of the error term is not constant across observations. The chapter illustrates it with a funnel-shaped residual plot in which the magnitude of residuals increases with fitted values. Constant variance is an assumption underlying the usual standard errors, confidence intervals, and hypothesis tests. When variance changes systematically, those inferential quantities may be unreliable. One possible remedy is to transform the response using a concave function such as a logarithm, which can reduce the relative spread of larger values. Another approach, when observation variances are known or can be estimated, is weighted least squares.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a residual-versus-fitted plot for a funnel shape and explain why it suggests changing error variance.
Key point:
- Heteroscedasticity means error variance changes across observations.
- It can undermine ordinary regression standard errors and inference.
45. Q: How does weighted least squares address non-constant error variance?
A: Show answer
Weighted least squares gives observations different weights when their error variances differ. The chapter explains that if a response is an average of ni uncorrelated raw observations with common variance, its variance is proportional to one over ni. In that setting, weights proportional to ni are appropriate because observations based on more raw measurements have lower variance. More generally, weights are chosen in relation to the inverse error variance. Weighted least squares can improve efficiency when the variance structure is understood, but it depends on having a defensible model for the weights rather than assigning them arbitrarily.
Study instruction:
- Explain why an average based on more independent measurements may receive a higher weight than an average based on fewer measurements.
Key point:
- Weighted least squares accounts for differing observation variances.
- Weights should reflect a justified variance model.
46. Q: What is an outlier in linear regression, and how can it affect a fitted model?
A: Show answer
An outlier is an observation whose response is far from the value predicted by the model. It may result from a recording error, unusual circumstances, or a model that omits an important predictor. An outlier with an ordinary predictor value may have a large residual but relatively little influence on the slope and intercept. It can nevertheless increase RSE and change R-squared, confidence intervals, and p-values because those quantities depend on residual variation. The chapter warns against automatically deleting outliers: the analyst should first investigate whether the observation is erroneous or whether it reveals a deficiency in the model or the data collection process.
Study instruction:
- Identify a large-residual observation and explain why it should be investigated before deciding whether to remove it.
Key point:
- Outliers have unusual response values relative to the fitted model.
- They can affect fit summaries even when they do not strongly move the regression line.
47. Q: What are studentized residuals, and how can they help identify possible outliers?
A: Show answer
A studentized residual divides a residual by an estimate of its standard error, putting the residual on a standardized scale. This makes observations easier to compare because the raw residual magnitude can depend on the model and observation. The chapter notes that absolute studentized residuals greater than about three are potential outlier signals. This is a diagnostic rule of thumb, not an automatic deletion rule or a formal proof that a point is invalid. A large studentized residual should trigger investigation of the observation, its measurement, the model form, and possible omitted predictors.
Study instruction:
- Calculate or inspect studentized residuals and identify observations that merit follow-up because their absolute values are unusually large.
Key point:
- Studentized residuals standardize residual size for diagnostic comparison.
- Large values flag possible outliers but require contextual review.
48. Q: What is a high-leverage observation, and how is it different from an outlier?
A: Show answer
An outlier is unusual in the response relative to the fitted model, while a high-leverage observation is unusual in its predictor values. A high-leverage point can strongly influence the fitted regression line or surface because it lies far from the center of the predictor distribution. An observation can be an outlier without having high leverage, a high-leverage point without a large residual, or both. The chapter illustrates that removing a high-leverage observation can change the regression line much more than removing an ordinary-predictor outlier. Analysts should examine both residual size and predictor-space location when assessing influential observations.
Study instruction:
- Classify four hypothetical observations by residual size and predictor unusualness, distinguishing outliers from high-leverage points.
Key point:
- Outliers are unusual in response space; high-leverage points are unusual in predictor space.
- The two concepts describe different diagnostic risks.
49. Q: What does the leverage statistic measure?
A: Show answer
The leverage statistic quantifies how unusual an observation's predictor values are relative to the overall predictor distribution. In simple regression it increases with the distance of the predictor value from its sample mean. In multiple regression it generalizes to the full predictor space, where a point can be unusual in a combination of variables even if no single variable is extreme. The statistic ranges between one over n and one, and the average leverage equals the number of fitted coefficients divided by n. Observations with leverage substantially above the average deserve investigation because they may have disproportionate influence on the fitted model.
Study instruction:
- Compare an observation's leverage with the average leverage for a model and explain why an unusually large value deserves attention.
Key point:
- Leverage measures unusualness in predictor space.
- High leverage can make a fitted model sensitive to a small number of observations.
50. Q: Why is the combination of a high-leverage point and a large residual especially concerning?
A: Show answer
A high-leverage point can pull the fitted regression line toward itself, while a large residual indicates that the model still predicts its response poorly. When both occur together, the observation may have a strong effect on the fitted coefficients and also be inconsistent with the fitted relationship. This combination can signal a serious data problem, an unusual case, or a model that fails to represent an important part of the predictor space. Diagnostic plots of studentized residuals against leverage help identify such observations. The analyst should investigate the case and assess how sensitive conclusions are to its presence rather than remove it automatically.
Study instruction:
- Locate observations with both large studentized residuals and high leverage on a diagnostic plot and describe the follow-up analysis they require.
Key point:
- High leverage and large residuals together can create influential observations.
- Sensitivity analysis and data validation are preferable to automatic deletion.
51. Q: What is collinearity in multiple regression?
A: Show answer
Collinearity occurs when two or more predictors are strongly related to one another. When predictors move together, it can be difficult to distinguish their separate associations with the response. The chapter's Credit example shows that credit limit and credit rating are highly correlated. A model containing both can estimate their combined contribution while producing unstable individual coefficient estimates. Collinearity does not necessarily prevent the model from predicting well, but it can make individual coefficients uncertain and reduce the power of tests that ask whether a particular predictor contributes after adjustment for the others.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a predictor correlation matrix and identify pairs that may create difficulty in separating their individual regression coefficients.
Key point:
- Collinearity means predictors contain overlapping information.
- It primarily complicates individual coefficient interpretation and inference.
52. Q: How does collinearity affect coefficient standard errors and hypothesis tests?
A: Show answer
Collinearity makes it harder to estimate the separate contribution of each correlated predictor. This increases the standard errors of the affected coefficient estimates and reduces their t-statistics. As a result, a predictor that is genuinely associated with the response may appear statistically insignificant once another highly correlated predictor is included. The overall model can still have a strong fit because the correlated predictors collectively carry useful information. Analysts should therefore distinguish the question of whether the predictor set is useful from the question of whether each individual coefficient can be estimated precisely.
Study instruction:
- Compare a model with two weakly correlated predictors to one with highly correlated predictors and explain how coefficient uncertainty can change even when overall fit remains similar.
Key point:
- Collinearity inflates coefficient uncertainty and can reduce statistical power.
- A non-significant individual coefficient does not always mean the predictor set lacks information.
53. Q: What is multicollinearity, and why can a pairwise correlation matrix fail to detect it?
A: Show answer
Multicollinearity is a dependence relationship involving three or more predictors. A pairwise correlation matrix may show no extremely high correlation between any two variables even though one predictor can be approximated by a combination of several others. This means that inspecting pairwise correlations is useful but not sufficient to rule out collinearity. The chapter introduces the variance inflation factor as a more direct way to assess how much the variance of a coefficient estimate is inflated by the other predictors. Analysts should examine the structure of the full predictor set rather than relying only on pairwise comparisons.
Study instruction:
- Explain how one predictor could be strongly predicted by a combination of two others even if no pairwise correlation is extreme.
Key point:
- Multicollinearity can involve combinations of several predictors.
- Pairwise correlations alone may miss it.
54. Q: What is the variance inflation factor, or VIF?
A: Show answer
The variance inflation factor measures how much the variance of a coefficient estimate is increased by the linear relationship between that predictor and the other predictors. A VIF of one indicates no collinearity for that predictor. Larger values indicate that the predictor can be explained more strongly by the remaining predictors and that its coefficient estimate may be less precise. The chapter offers a rule of thumb that values above about five or ten can indicate a problematic level of collinearity. These thresholds are not universal laws; the substantive consequences and the purpose of the model should guide interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Interpret VIF values of 1, 3, 8, and 20 and explain which ones merit closer investigation under the chapter's rule of thumb.
Key point:
- VIF quantifies inflation in coefficient variance due to predictor collinearity.
- Large VIF values signal uncertainty in individual coefficient estimates.
55. Q: What are two practical ways to address collinearity?
A: Show answer
One approach is to remove one of the highly correlated predictors, especially when it contributes largely redundant information. Another is to combine related predictors into a single measure, for example by averaging standardized versions of variables that represent a common underlying concept. The chapter's Credit example shows that dropping one of two strongly collinear variables can greatly reduce VIF values while causing only a small change in R-squared. The choice should be based on the scientific or business meaning of the predictors, not solely on a numerical threshold. Removing a variable can change the question being answered, so the analyst should explain the rationale and evaluate the consequences.
Study instruction:
- Propose two ways to address a pair of collinear predictors and compare the interpretability and information lost under each approach.
Key point:
- Redundant predictors can be removed or combined.
- Any remedy should preserve the meaning of the analysis.
56. Q: How does K-nearest neighbors regression differ from K-nearest neighbors classification?
A: Show answer
Both methods identify the K training observations closest to a new predictor value. KNN classification estimates class probabilities from the proportions of neighbors in each class and assigns the most common class. KNN regression instead estimates the response by averaging the quantitative response values of the K nearest neighbors. The method is non-parametric because it does not assume a fixed linear form for the relationship. Its prediction is local: it depends on the observations near the target predictor point. The two methods share the idea of neighborhood-based learning but differ in the type of response they produce and the way the neighborhood information is summarized.
Study instruction:
- Use the same set of neighbors to calculate a class prediction and a numerical response prediction, then explain the difference in the aggregation step.
Key point:
- KNN classification predicts a class; KNN regression predicts an average response.
- Both rely on distances to training observations.
57. Q: How does the value of K affect KNN regression?
A: Show answer
With a small K, the prediction depends on only a few nearby observations, producing a highly flexible and locally variable fit. With a larger K, the prediction averages more responses, creating a smoother fit that is less sensitive to individual observations. A small K tends to have lower bias and higher variance; a larger K tends to have higher bias and lower variance. The chapter illustrates K equal to one producing a step function that interpolates the training observations, while K equal to nine produces a smoother function. The optimal K depends on the relationship, sample size, and noise and should be selected using an estimate of test error.
Study instruction:
- Compare K equal to one and K equal to nine on a small data set and describe how the fitted prediction changes as the neighborhood expands.
Key point:
- Small K produces flexible, high-variance predictions.
- Large K smooths predictions but may introduce bias.
58. Q: When can linear regression outperform KNN regression?
A: Show answer
If the true relationship is close to linear, a parametric linear model can estimate it efficiently using a relatively small number of coefficients. KNN may introduce unnecessary variance by relying on local neighborhoods, even though a straight line would describe the relationship well. The chapter's simulated linear example shows least squares outperforming KNN in test MSE. Linear regression also has an interpretability advantage because the fitted relationship can be summarized with coefficients and standard errors. If the true relationship is strongly non-linear, however, KNN may outperform a restrictive linear model, provided enough observations are available in the relevant predictor space.
Study instruction:
- Compare a nearly linear data-generating relationship with a strongly non-linear one and justify which method may perform better in each setting.
Key point:
- A correct parametric form can outperform a flexible non-parametric method.
- KNN becomes attractive when local non-linearity is important and data are sufficient.
59. Q: What is the curse of dimensionality in KNN regression?
A: Show answer
As the number of predictors increases, observations become spread across a higher-dimensional space. Even when the training sample size stays fixed, the nearest K observations may be far from the target point. Their responses may therefore provide poor local information about the target relationship. The chapter demonstrates that adding irrelevant noise predictors can sharply worsen KNN performance because the effective local sample density falls as dimension increases. Linear regression may deteriorate more slowly in the same example because it estimates a structured parametric relationship. The problem is especially serious when there are few observations relative to the number of predictors.
Study instruction:
- Explain why 100 observations can provide useful neighborhoods in one dimension but very poor neighborhoods in a space with many predictors.
Key point:
- High dimensionality makes nearby observations sparse.
- KNN can degrade rapidly as predictor count grows relative to sample size.
60. Q: What should be considered when choosing between linear regression and KNN in a real application?
A: Show answer
The analyst should consider the likely shape of the relationship, the number of observations relative to predictors, the level of noise, the desired interpretability, and the estimated test performance of each method. Linear regression is attractive when a linear form is plausible, when the sample is limited relative to dimension, or when coefficient interpretation matters. KNN may be useful when the relationship is non-linear and the data provide enough nearby observations. The chapter cautions that KNN is not automatically safer simply because it is flexible: it can perform poorly in high dimensions. Method choice should be empirical and aligned with the decision being supported.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a decision table comparing linear regression and KNN across functional form, sample size, dimensionality, interpretability, and test error.
Key point:
- No method is universally superior.
- Model choice balances structure, data density, predictive accuracy, and interpretability.
61. Q: How does the R lab fit a simple linear regression model using lm()?
A: Show answer
The lab uses lm(response ~ predictor, data = dataset) to fit a simple least squares regression. The data argument identifies the data frame containing the variables and avoids ambiguity about where the response and predictor are stored. The fitted object can be printed for basic coefficient information, while summary() provides standard errors, t-statistics, p-values, R-squared, residual standard error, and the F-statistic. The lab's Boston example predicts median home value using the percentage of lower-status households as a predictor. The syntax is compact, but the analyst must confirm that the response and predictor are correctly specified and that the model's assumptions are checked after fitting.
Study instruction:
- Fit a simple regression with lm(), then use summary() and interpret the coefficient, p-value, R-squared, and residual standard error.
Key point:
- lm() fits the regression and summary() provides inferential and fit information.
- The data argument makes variable provenance explicit.
62. Q: How can R extract coefficients and confidence intervals from a fitted regression?
A: Show answer
The lab demonstrates coef() to extract coefficient estimates and confint() to calculate confidence intervals. It notes that although individual elements can be accessed by name from the fitted object, extractor functions provide a safer and clearer interface. Confidence intervals use the estimated coefficient and its standard error to express uncertainty about the population parameter. Analysts should check which coefficient is being reported and retain the units and reference categories needed for interpretation. A confidence interval that includes zero indicates that the data do not provide strong evidence of a non-zero coefficient at the corresponding two-sided level under the model assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Use coef() and confint() on a fitted model and write an interpretation for one coefficient interval in the context of the response.
Key point:
- coef() extracts estimates; confint() summarizes coefficient uncertainty.
- Coefficient intervals should be interpreted with the model's assumptions and units.
63. Q: How does predict() produce confidence and prediction intervals in R?
A: Show answer
The predict() function can generate fitted responses for specified predictor values and can request either a confidence interval or a prediction interval. A confidence interval describes uncertainty about the average response at those predictor values, while a prediction interval describes uncertainty about an individual future response. The lab shows that both intervals are centered on the same fitted value but the prediction interval is much wider because it includes individual-level error variation. Analysts should specify the new predictor values in a data frame with the same variable names expected by the model and clearly state which interval type is being reported.
Study instruction:
- Use predict() with interval set to confidence and prediction for the same new predictor value, then compare the widths and explain why they differ.
Key point:
- predict() can return fitted values and uncertainty intervals.
- Prediction intervals are wider than confidence intervals for the same inputs.
64. Q: Which R diagnostic functions in the lab help assess residuals and leverage?
A: Show answer
The lab uses residuals() to extract ordinary residuals, rstudent() to obtain studentized residuals, and hatvalues() to calculate leverage statistics. Plotting residuals against fitted values helps identify non-linearity or unequal variance. Studentized residual plots help flag observations with unusually large response deviations, while leverage plots help identify observations with unusual predictor combinations. which.max() can identify the index of the largest leverage value. These tools do not make the diagnostic decision automatically; they provide evidence for the analyst to investigate the data, model assumptions, and sensitivity of conclusions to unusual cases.
Study instruction:
- Fit a regression and produce plots of residuals, studentized residuals, and leverage values; identify observations that require further investigation.
Key point:
- Residual and leverage functions support model diagnostics.
- Unusual values should be investigated in context rather than automatically deleted.
65. Q: How does R specify multiple regression, interactions, and non-linear terms?
A: Show answer
The lab uses plus signs in a model formula to include multiple predictors. A dot can represent all remaining variables in the data frame, and a minus term can remove a predictor from that set. The colon operator includes an interaction term only, while the asterisk includes both main effects and their interaction. I(X^2) adds a squared predictor term while preventing the formula language from treating the exponent symbol as a special operator. poly(X, degree) creates polynomial terms of a specified degree. update() can modify an existing model formula. Understanding these formula operators makes it easier to specify and compare models without manually constructing every transformed variable.
Study instruction:
- Write R formulas for a two-predictor additive model, an interaction model, a quadratic model, and a full model excluding one variable.
Key point:
- R formulas provide concise ways to specify predictors and interactions.
- I() and poly() support polynomial transformations.
66. Q: How can an analyst compare nested regression models using anova()?
A: Show answer
The lab uses anova() to compare a smaller model with a larger nested model, such as a linear model against a quadratic model that adds a squared predictor. The comparison tests whether the additional terms improve fit enough to justify the larger model under the test's assumptions. The output includes an F-statistic and p-value for the change in residual sum of squares. A small p-value provides evidence that the larger model fits better than the restricted model. The comparison does not by itself establish that the more complex model predicts better on new data, so the result should be considered alongside diagnostics and an estimate of test error.
Study instruction:
- Fit a linear and quadratic model to the same response and predictor, then use anova() to compare their residual sums of squares and interpret the result.
Key point:
- anova() can test whether added terms improve a nested model's fit.
- Improved training fit should still be checked for generalization and overfitting.
67. Q: How are qualitative predictors handled automatically by R in the Carseats lab?
A: Show answer
When a factor is included in an lm() formula, R creates dummy variables according to its contrast coding. For a three-level shelving-location variable, the lab uses one category as the baseline and creates indicators for the other two categories. Their coefficients describe differences in sales relative to the baseline category, holding the other predictors fixed. contrasts() shows the coding used by R. Analysts should inspect the baseline and factor levels before interpreting coefficients because the reference category determines how the estimates are expressed. The fitted predictions remain meaningful across categories even though different contrast systems can produce different coefficient representations.
Study instruction:
- Fit a model containing a multi-level factor, inspect contrasts(), and explain each coefficient relative to the omitted baseline category.
Key point:
- R uses factor contrasts to encode qualitative predictors.
- Baseline choice determines coefficient interpretation.
68. Q: Why does the lab demonstrate writing a custom R function?
A: Show answer
The lab shows that analysts can define their own function when a repeated operation is not already available as a built-in function or package function. Its LoadLibraries() example groups several library() calls and a printed message into one reusable command. A custom function reduces repeated typing, encourages consistent procedures, and makes code easier to organize. The function must be defined before it can be called, and the body is enclosed in braces. The example is simple, but the principle generalizes to reusable data preparation, diagnostic, and reporting procedures. A custom function should have a clear purpose and be tested before it is relied on in a larger analysis.
Study instruction:
- Write a short R function that performs a repeatable preparation or reporting task and test it on a small example.
Key point:
- Custom functions package repeated operations into reusable code.
- Define and test a function before relying on it in an analysis.
69. Q: How should an analyst translate the chapter's seven Advertising questions into a defensible marketing recommendation?
A: Show answer
The analyst should first assess whether advertising budgets are associated with sales, then quantify the strength of the relationship using measures such as RSE and R-squared. Multiple regression helps estimate the conditional association of each medium, while coefficient intervals express uncertainty. Residual diagnostics assess whether the additive linear form is adequate, and an interaction term can capture synergy between media. Predictions for average sales should use confidence intervals, while forecasts for a particular market should use prediction intervals. The recommendation should distinguish statistical association from causal effect and should not interpret the newspaper coefficient from a separate simple regression as its independent contribution when radio and TV are also relevant.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short marketing recommendation that addresses all seven questions and cites the relevant coefficient, model-fit, interval, and diagnostic evidence.
Key point:
- A defensible recommendation combines inference, prediction, uncertainty, and diagnostics.
- Conditional effects and possible interactions must be considered before allocating a budget.
70. Q: What common interpretation errors should be avoided when reading linear regression output?
A: Show answer
Analysts should not equate a small p-value with a large practical effect, a high R-squared with a valid model, or a non-significant coefficient with proof that no relationship exists. They should not interpret a multiple-regression coefficient as an unadjusted association, confuse a confidence interval for the mean with a prediction interval for an individual case, or treat an outlier as synonymous with a high-leverage point. They should also avoid assuming that adding predictors necessarily improves test performance, that pairwise correlations reveal every multicollinearity problem, or that a fitted linear form is automatically adequate. Each statistic answers a specific question and depends on assumptions that should be checked.
Study instruction:
- Review a sample regression report and identify at least five statements that confuse significance, fit, prediction, or diagnostics.
Key point:
- Regression output must be interpreted according to the question each statistic answers.
- Model assumptions and practical context remain essential.
Rows: 70
Table 4
Classification: Logistic Regression, Discriminant Analysis, and K-Nearest Neighbors
1. Q: What is classification, and how does it differ from regression?
A: Show answer
Classification is a supervised learning task in which the response is qualitative or categorical, such as default versus no default, fraud versus legitimate, or one of several medical diagnoses. A classifier uses predictor information from labelled training observations to assign a new observation to a class. Many methods first estimate class probabilities and then convert those probabilities into a class decision. Regression instead predicts a quantitative response, such as income or temperature. The distinction concerns the nature of the response, not whether predictors are numeric or categorical.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why predicting a customer's default status is classification while predicting the amount of the customer's debt is regression."
Key point:
- Classification predicts categories, while regression predicts quantities.
- Class probabilities can underpin a classification decision.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
2. Q: What are the main stages of a supervised classification problem?
A: Show answer
A classification workflow begins by defining the target classes and the population or decision context. The analyst then assembles labelled observations, selects predictors available at the time a decision would be made, and separates data for model development from data for evaluation. A classifier is fitted on training data, its predictions are assessed on unseen observations, and the resulting probabilities or labels are interpreted using an explicit decision rule. Deployment also requires monitoring for changing data, class prevalence, error costs, and data quality.
Study instruction:
- "Map a fraud-detection project from target definition through independent testing and post-deployment monitoring."
Key point:
- A valid test must represent genuinely unseen cases.
- The target definition and timing of predictors must match the intended use.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
3. Q: Why should a classifier be evaluated on observations not used to fit it?
A: Show answer
Training performance measures how well a fitted model describes the data used to estimate it. It can be overly optimistic because the fitting procedure has already adapted to those observations. The practical objective is performance on future or otherwise unseen cases, so an independent test set provides a more realistic estimate of generalization. The split must be made before model fitting and any data-dependent preprocessing; otherwise information from the test set can leak into training and make evaluation misleading.
Study instruction:
- "Identify every step in a modeling pipeline that must be fitted using training data only."
Key point:
- Training fit is not a reliable substitute for test performance.
- Keep the test set independent of fitting and preprocessing decisions.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
4. Q: What is a classifier, and why might it estimate probabilities before assigning a class?
A: Show answer
A classifier is a rule or fitted procedure that maps predictor values to one of the response categories. A probability-based classifier estimates the conditional probability of each class given the predictors, then assigns a label using a decision threshold or a maximum-probability rule. Probabilities retain information about uncertainty and allow the decision threshold to reflect the consequences of different errors. A hard class label alone hides whether the model was nearly indifferent between classes or strongly favored one of them.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two predicted cases with probabilities of 0.51 and 0.98 and explain why their risk interpretations differ."
Key point:
- A predicted label can conceal substantial uncertainty.
- Probability estimates support decisions beyond simple maximum-probability assignment.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
5. Q: Why is ordinary linear regression generally unsuitable for a qualitative response with more than two unordered classes?
A: Show answer
Assigning arbitrary numeric codes to unordered categories creates artificial numerical distances and ordering. For example, coding diagnoses as 1, 2, and 3 implies that one diagnosis lies between the others and that the gaps are comparable, although neither statement has a substantive basis. Changing the arbitrary codes can change the fitted regression and predictions. A method designed for categorical outcomes avoids making conclusions depend on an arbitrary coding of class names.
Study instruction:
- "Construct two different numeric codings for three unordered categories and explain why the implied order is invalid."
Key point:
- Nominal categories do not have meaningful numeric spacing.
- Arbitrary coding can alter fitted predictions.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
6. Q: What limitations arise when linear regression is fitted to a binary 0/1 response?
A: Show answer
For a binary response coded zero and one, the conditional mean is mathematically equal to the probability of the class coded one. A least-squares line can therefore provide a rough ordering, but its fitted values are not constrained to the interval from zero to one. Values below zero or above one cannot be interpreted as probabilities. The linear form also imposes a constant probability change per predictor unit, whereas the true probability response may flatten near zero and one. These limitations motivate logistic regression.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a predicted value of 1.12 cannot be accepted as a probability and why clipping it does not solve the model specification problem."
Key point:
- Binary least squares may produce invalid probability values.
- Use a probability model designed for bounded outcomes.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
7. Q: What is the logistic function in binary logistic regression?
A: Show answer
The logistic function transforms a linear predictor into a value strictly between zero and one. In a one-predictor model, the linear predictor is the intercept plus the predictor multiplied by its coefficient; applying the logistic transformation produces the estimated probability of the event. The resulting curve is S-shaped: probabilities approach zero at very negative linear predictor values and approach one at very positive values. This bounded output makes the model suitable for representing a binary conditional probability.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch the S-shaped relationship and identify its lower and upper limiting values."
Key point:
- The logistic transformation maps any real-valued linear predictor to a valid probability.
- The probability response is nonlinear even though the logit is linear.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
8. Q: What are odds, and how are they related to probability?
A: Show answer
For an event with probability p, the odds are p divided by one minus p. Probability is bounded between zero and one, whereas odds range from zero upward without an upper bound. A probability of 0.20 corresponds to odds of 0.25, or one event for every four non-events. A probability of 0.90 corresponds to odds of 9. Odds and probability describe the same event from different perspectives, but they are not interchangeable and should not be reported as if they were the same quantity.
Study instruction:
- "Convert probabilities of 0.10, 0.50, and 0.80 into odds and explain each result in words."
Key point:
- Odds compare event probability with non-event probability.
- Do not describe an odds ratio as a probability ratio.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
9. Q: What is the logit, and why is it central to logistic regression?
A: Show answer
The logit is the natural logarithm of the odds, log[p/(1-p)]. Logistic regression assumes that this transformed probability is a linear function of the predictors. This formulation allows the linear predictor to take any real value while the inverse-logit transformation keeps the fitted probability between zero and one. It also gives coefficients a convenient interpretation on the log-odds scale. The linearity assumption applies to the logit, not directly to the probability itself.
Study instruction:
- "Write the relationship between probability, odds, and log-odds and identify which quantity is modeled linearly."
Key point:
- The logit is the logarithm of the odds.
- Linearity is assumed on the log-odds scale.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
10. Q: How should a logistic regression coefficient be interpreted?
A: Show answer
In a logistic regression model, a one-unit increase in a predictor changes the log-odds of the modeled event by that predictor's coefficient, holding other included predictors constant. Exponentiating the coefficient gives an odds multiplier for a one-unit increase. A positive coefficient indicates increasing event odds as the predictor increases; a negative coefficient indicates decreasing odds. The coefficient is not a fixed change in probability because the probability change depends on the starting probability and the values of the other predictors.
Study instruction:
- "For a coefficient of 0.4, calculate the odds multiplier using exp(0.4) and describe the interpretation without calling it a probability increase."
Key point:
- Coefficients operate on log-odds.
- Exponentiated coefficients are odds ratios, conditional on the other model terms.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
11. Q: Why is a logistic coefficient not the same as a constant change in probability?
A: Show answer
The inverse-logit curve is nonlinear, so the probability effect of a predictor depends on the current linear predictor. The same coefficient can produce a small probability change near the lower or upper tail and a larger change around the middle of the curve. Consequently, a coefficient should first be interpreted on the log-odds scale. If a decision-maker needs probability differences, calculate predicted probabilities for clearly specified predictor profiles or report marginal effects with their assumptions and uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate predicted probabilities at two starting profiles before describing the practical impact of a one-unit predictor change."
Key point:
- A constant log-odds effect does not imply a constant probability effect.
- Use predicted probabilities for concrete case-level interpretation.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
12. Q: How are logistic regression coefficients estimated?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses maximum likelihood rather than ordinary least squares. The likelihood measures how plausible the observed binary outcomes are under a proposed set of model coefficients and their implied probabilities. Estimation searches for coefficient values that maximize this likelihood, assigning high fitted probabilities to observed events and low fitted probabilities to observed non-events. The resulting estimates depend on the observed training data and model specification. Convergence, sparse data, and separation can affect whether stable finite estimates are obtained.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how an observed event with a very low fitted probability affects the likelihood compared with a well-predicted event."
Key point:
- Maximum likelihood chooses coefficients that make observed outcomes most plausible under the model.
- Model convergence and data adequacy still require review.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
13. Q: What is the role of the z-statistic and p-value in logistic regression output?
A: Show answer
A coefficient's standard error describes the estimated sampling uncertainty under the fitted model assumptions. The z-statistic compares the coefficient estimate with its standard error under a null hypothesis, commonly that the coefficient equals zero. A small p-value indicates that the observed estimate would be unusual under that null model, but it does not measure the size or practical importance of the effect. Statistical significance also does not establish causation, predictive usefulness, or adequate model calibration.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret a small p-value alongside the coefficient magnitude, confidence interval, and practical context."
Key point:
- Statistical significance is not the same as practical importance.
- A p-value does not establish causality or prediction quality.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
14. Q: How does logistic regression make a binary class prediction?
A: Show answer
After estimating coefficients, the model calculates a linear predictor for a new case and transforms it into an estimated event probability. A classification rule then compares that probability with a chosen threshold. With a threshold of 0.5, a case is assigned to the event class when its estimated probability exceeds 0.5; otherwise it is assigned to the other class. The threshold is a decision choice, not a mathematical requirement of logistic regression, and it should reflect the problem's error costs and operational purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Use a probability of 0.42 to show how the assigned class changes when the threshold changes from 0.50 to 0.30."
Key point:
- A model probability and a final class decision are separate outputs.
- The threshold must be chosen for the decision context.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
15. Q: Why might a classification threshold below 0.5 be appropriate?
A: Show answer
A lower threshold can be appropriate when failing to identify a true event is more costly than investigating some additional false alarms. The chapter illustrates that a conservative credit-risk screen might use a probability threshold below 0.5 to identify more potentially risky cases. Lowering the threshold generally increases the number classified as positive, which can increase sensitivity while also increasing false positives. The threshold should be evaluated against staffing capacity, intervention cost, and the consequences of missed events rather than selected automatically.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the operational consequences of thresholds 0.50 and 0.20 in a fraud review process."
Key point:
- The 0.5 threshold is not universally optimal.
- Threshold selection trades off different kinds of classification error.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
16. Q: What is multiple logistic regression?
A: Show answer
Multiple logistic regression models a binary response using more than one predictor. The logit of the event probability is expressed as an intercept plus a weighted combination of predictor values. Each coefficient describes the association between its predictor and event log-odds while holding the other included predictors fixed. This can help account for several relevant characteristics simultaneously, but it does not automatically remove confounding, guarantee causality, or protect against omitted variables and poor functional form.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret a balance coefficient in a model that also includes income, making clear that the other modeled predictor is held constant."
Key point:
- Multiple predictors enter the linear predictor together.
- Each coefficient is conditional on the other included predictors.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
17. Q: How should categorical predictors be incorporated into logistic regression?
A: Show answer
A categorical predictor is represented through indicator or contrast variables relative to a reference category. The fitted coefficients compare each represented category with that reference while holding the other model terms fixed. The reference level and contrast coding affect the way coefficients are displayed, although fitted comparisons can be re-expressed under another valid coding. Analysts should check factor levels, sparse categories, and whether the reference category is substantively meaningful before interpreting results.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a reference category for a three-level account type variable and explain the meaning of one contrast coefficient."
Key point:
- Categorical predictors require an explicit coding scheme.
- Coefficient interpretation depends on the reference level and contrasts.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
18. Q: What is an interaction in multiple logistic regression?
A: Show answer
An interaction allows the association between one predictor and the log-odds outcome to depend on the value or category of another predictor. Without an interaction, the model imposes an additive structure on the logit for the specified terms. An interaction can represent a plausible effect pattern, such as a balance-risk association that differs by customer segment, but it increases model complexity and should be supported by adequate data and a clear rationale. Interpret predicted probabilities across combinations rather than reading an interaction coefficient in isolation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare predicted probabilities for low and high balance across two customer groups to explain a possible interaction."
Key point:
- Interactions represent non-additive predictor relationships on the modeled scale.
- Include them for a substantive reason and evaluate their stability.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- R stats: glm documentation
19. Q: How does logistic regression extend to more than two response classes?
A: Show answer
The chapter introduces logistic regression for multiple response classes through a multinomial formulation. Instead of estimating only one event probability, the model represents the relative probabilities of the available categories while ensuring that the class probabilities form a coherent distribution. The model can compare each class with a reference category through log-odds contrasts. It is suitable for unordered categories, unlike assigning arbitrary numeric scores to class labels. The number of parameters and interpretation complexity increase as classes are added.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how a three-diagnosis problem differs from a binary default problem in its probability outputs."
Key point:
- Multiclass logistic regression handles unordered response categories.
- The estimated class probabilities must be interpreted jointly.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
20. Q: What is the generative approach to classification described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
A generative classifier models the distribution of predictor values separately within each response class, together with the prior probability of each class. Bayes' theorem then combines these ingredients to estimate the probability that an observation belongs to a class given its predictor values. This reverses the direct conditional modeling strategy used by logistic regression. The approach depends on assumptions about the class-specific predictor distributions, and its quality depends on how well those assumptions describe the data.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast modeling predictors conditional on class with directly modeling class probability conditional on predictors."
Key point:
- Generative methods model class-conditional predictor distributions.
- Bayes' theorem converts those distributions into posterior class probabilities.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
- CRAN MASS reference manual: lda and qda
21. Q: How does Bayes' theorem support classification?
A: Show answer
For class k, Bayes' theorem combines the prior probability of that class with the likelihood or density of the observed predictor values under that class, then divides by the overall predictor density. The result is the posterior probability of class membership given the observed predictors. The prior represents prevalence before observing the predictors; the class-conditional density represents how compatible the observed predictors are with that class. Posterior probabilities therefore depend on both prevalence and how distinctive the predictors are within each class.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a rare event can still have a high posterior probability for a case with highly distinctive predictor values."
Key point:
- Posterior probability combines prior prevalence and class-conditional evidence.
- Neither the prior nor the likelihood alone is the final classification probability.
Source links:
- James et al., Chapter 4: Classification
22. Q: What is a prior probability in classification?
A: Show answer
The prior probability for a class is the probability that a randomly selected observation belongs to that class before the current predictor values are considered. In a representative training sample, it can be estimated by the proportion of observations in that class. In some applications, external knowledge about the target population may justify a different prior. The prior matters because it affects posterior probabilities and decision boundaries; using a training sample with an altered class composition without adjustment can produce misleading probability estimates for the target population.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate class priors from a training sample and discuss when the sample proportions might not represent deployment prevalence."
Key point:
- Priors describe class prevalence before case-specific predictor evidence.
- Check whether the training sample reflects the population where the model will be used.
Source links:
