- "Classify several analytics tasks as supervised or unsupervised and state whether each seeks prediction, dimension reduction, or subgroup discovery."
Key point:
- Unsupervised learning uses features without a supplied response.
- Its purpose is to reveal structure rather than predict a known outcome.
2. Q: Why is unsupervised learning difficult to validate objectively?
A: Show answer
In supervised learning, predictions can be checked against known responses for observations not used in fitting. In unsupervised learning there is no equivalent target, so a plausible-looking cluster may reflect noise and a PCA plot may show variation without scientific meaning. There is no universal cross-validation or independent-test procedure that resolves every problem. Analysts instead combine domain knowledge, interpretability, stability under perturbation, sensitivity to modeling choices, and independent evidence where available. The absence of a single score changes the standard of evidence; it does not make exploratory methods useless.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast held-out prediction evaluation with the evidence needed to support an unsupervised finding."
Key point:
- There is no universal ground-truth score for unsupervised results.
- Interpretability and stability must supplement numerical summaries.
3. Q: What kinds of problems can unsupervised learning help investigate?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes cancer research, retail, and search behavior. Researchers may seek subgroups among patient samples or genes; retailers may group shoppers with similar purchase histories; search engines may use similar click patterns to organize or personalize results. These tasks discover structure without using known response labels. A discovered group is a hypothesis about the data, not proof of a permanent natural category. Its usefulness depends on the measurements, similarity definition, and whether it informs a meaningful scientific or business question.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a dataset and identify the observations, features, and practical question that grouping might inform."
Key point:
- Unsupervised methods can reveal latent patterns in complex datasets.
- Discovered patterns need domain interpretation before action.
4. Q: What is principal components analysis, and what problem does it solve?
A: Show answer
PCA constructs a smaller set of derived variables from the original features. Each component is a linear combination of the original variables, ordered so that the first captures the greatest possible variance, the second captures the greatest remaining variance subject to being uncorrelated with the first, and so on. PCA is useful when many correlated features make visualization or interpretation difficult. It can summarize observations, show relationships among variables, and provide reduced predictors for later analysis. It does not use an outcome and does not guarantee that high-variance directions are the most predictive directions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how PCA replaces many original variables with a smaller set of linear combinations and identify the purpose of the reduction."
Key point:
- PCA summarizes many features through ordered components.
- Components maximize variance, not necessarily predictive relevance.
5. Q: How is the first principal component defined?
A: Show answer
The first principal component is the normalized linear combination of the original features with the largest sample variance. Its coefficients, called loadings, form a vector whose squared elements sum to one; otherwise the variance could be made arbitrarily large by scaling the coefficients. The variables are centered before the component is computed. PC1 therefore identifies the direction in feature space along which the observations vary most. It is a data-driven summary, not necessarily one original feature and not automatically a causal factor.
Study instruction:
- "State the variance-maximization objective and explain why the loading vector must be normalized."
Key point:
- PC1 is the unit-length linear combination with maximum variance.
- Its loadings define the direction of greatest variation in centered data.
6. Q: What is the difference between PCA loadings and PCA scores?
A: Show answer
Loadings are the coefficients that define a component as a linear combination of the original features. Scores are the resulting component values for each observation after projecting its feature measurements onto the loading direction. With n observations and p features, each loading vector has length p and each score vector has length n. Loadings help interpret which variables contribute to a component; scores locate observations in the component coordinate system. Both are needed to understand a biplot and the transformed data.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the loading vector and describe how one centered observation is projected to obtain its score."
Key point:
- Loadings describe a component in feature space.
- Scores describe observations in the component coordinate system.
7. Q: Why are variables centered before PCA?
A: Show answer
PCA seeks directions that maximize variation, so the data should be analyzed around each feature's mean rather than around an arbitrary origin. Centering subtracts each feature mean, making each column mean zero. The resulting components describe variation around the data center. In the chapter's formulation, centered columns are assumed; R's prcomp centers variables by default and stores the means used. Centering is distinct from scaling: centering changes location, while scaling changes the relative influence of variables according to their units and variances.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between subtracting a mean and dividing by a standard deviation."
Key point:
- Centering makes each feature mean zero before PCA.
- Centering and scaling address different preprocessing issues.
8. Q: How are the second and later principal components determined?
A: Show answer
After PC1, PC2 is the normalized linear combination with the largest remaining variance among directions uncorrelated with PC1. In loading space, this is equivalent to requiring its loading vector to be orthogonal to the first. Later components are found in the same way, maximizing remaining variance while being orthogonal to all earlier directions. The components are therefore ordered and mutually uncorrelated. In two dimensions, once the first direction is fixed, only one perpendicular direction remains; in higher dimensions, additional orthogonal directions are available.
Study instruction:
- "Draw two perpendicular loading directions and explain why their score vectors are uncorrelated."
Key point:
- Components are ordered by the variance they explain.
- Later components are constrained by orthogonality to earlier directions.
9. Q: What is the geometric interpretation of principal components?
A: Show answer
Each loading vector defines a direction in the original feature space, and each score is an observation's projection onto that direction. PC1 points along the direction of greatest variation. The first two components span the plane with the greatest variance, providing a two-dimensional view of the data. This is useful for visualization because the directions are selected from the data geometry rather than from arbitrary pairs of original variables. A projection discards information when fewer than all components are retained, so a PCA plot is a compressed view, not a complete representation.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a data cloud, a component direction, and perpendicular projections of observations."
Key point:
- Scores are projections onto component directions.
- A low-dimensional PCA plot is a compressed representation.
10. Q: What does a PCA biplot display?
A: Show answer
A biplot displays observation scores and variable loading vectors together. Observation labels show where cases lie on the first component axes, while arrows indicate how original variables load on those components. In the USArrests example, the crime variables point in similar directions on PC1, while UrbanPop contributes strongly to PC2. This makes it possible to interpret patterns among observations and relationships among variables in one graphic. The display scaling affects the exact visual interpretation, so its convention should be checked. A biplot is a visualization aid, not proof of causation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify observation coordinates and variable-arrow directions on a biplot and explain what each represents."
Key point:
- A biplot combines scores and loadings.
- Interpret arrows and points according to the plot's scaling convention.
11. Q: How can PCA be interpreted as finding the best low-dimensional approximation?
A: Show answer
The first M components span the M-dimensional linear subspace closest to the observations when closeness is measured by average squared Euclidean distance. PC1 defines the best-fitting line, the first two define the best-fitting plane, and additional components define higher-dimensional subspaces. Projecting observations onto the subspace approximates their original feature vectors. Retaining more components improves reconstruction; retaining all available components gives an exact representation within the centered data's effective rank. This connects variance maximization with minimum squared reconstruction error.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why maximizing projected variance is related to minimizing squared perpendicular reconstruction distances."
Key point:
- The first M components give the best M-dimensional linear approximation under squared Euclidean distance.
- More retained components improve reconstruction.
12. Q: Why can scaling variables change PCA results substantially?
A: Show answer
PCA is based on variance, so variables with larger numerical variances can dominate the selected directions. If features use different units, a large variance may reflect measurement scale rather than substantive importance. In USArrests, Assault has far greater variance than the other variables, so unscaled PC1 is driven largely by Assault. After standardizing variables to unit standard deviation, the first component reflects the crime variables more evenly and the second captures urbanization. Standardization is generally recommended when units differ, but the decision remains application-dependent.
Study instruction:
- "Compare feature variances and explain how a large numerical scale can dominate a loading vector."
Key point:
- PCA is sensitive to scale because it maximizes variance.
- Standardization prevents arbitrary units from dominating when scales are incomparable.
13. Q: When might it be reasonable not to scale variables before PCA?
A: Show answer
Scaling is not automatic. If variables share the same units and their variance differences are substantively meaningful, standardizing each to unit variance may remove information the analyst wants to preserve. The chapter gives gene-expression measurements as an example where all variables may share units, so an analyst might choose not to scale every gene. The decision depends on whether larger-variance features should have greater influence or whether each feature should contribute on a comparable standardized scale. The choice should be documented because it can materially change the components.
Study instruction:
- "For a dataset, document whether variables share units and decide whether raw variance or equalized scales better match the analysis goal."
Key point:
- Standardization is a substantive modeling choice, not a mechanical rule.
- The scale decision should reflect units and purpose.
14. Q: Why can software packages return principal components with opposite signs?
A: Show answer
A loading vector defines a direction, and multiplying every element by negative one leaves that direction unchanged. The associated score vector also changes sign, but the product of scores and loadings remains the same, so the reconstructed representation is unchanged. PCA components are therefore unique only up to a sign flip. Different packages can return mirrored loadings and scores while representing the same solution. The sign has no intrinsic meaning; the relative loading pattern and score relationships matter.
Study instruction:
- "Flip the sign of both a loading vector and its score vector and verify that the reconstruction is unchanged."
Key point:
- Component signs are arbitrary up to a simultaneous flip.
- A sign reversal does not indicate a different PCA solution.
15. Q: What is the proportion of variance explained by a principal component?
A: Show answer
PVE is the variance captured by one component divided by total variance in the centered dataset. The component variance is its eigenvalue, or the square of the component standard deviation reported by prcomp. Cumulative PVE is the sum of the individual proportions for the first M components. All available component PVEs sum to one. In USArrests, PC1 explains about 62.0 percent and PC2 about 24.7 percent, so the first two together explain nearly 87 percent of total variance.
Study instruction:
- "Square component standard deviations, divide by total variance, and calculate cumulative PVE."
Key point:
- PVE measures the share of total variance captured by a component.
- Cumulative PVE summarizes the variance retained by several components.
16. Q: How is a scree plot used to choose the number of components?
A: Show answer
A scree plot displays the PVE of each component and helps identify where additional components contribute progressively less variation. Analysts often look for an elbow, after which PVE drops off, and retain a small number before that decline. A cumulative PVE plot shows the total variation retained as components are added. The chapter emphasizes that exploratory selection is subjective: there is no universally accepted cutoff. The number retained should depend on the purpose, patterns revealed, application, and whether later components contain interpretable structure.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a plausible elbow and explain why it is a judgment rather than a universal threshold."
Key point:
- Scree plots reveal diminishing returns from additional components.
- Exploratory component retention requires context and judgment.
17. Q: Why can component count be selected more objectively in supervised analysis?
A: Show answer
In exploratory PCA, there is no response variable to optimize. For supervised tasks such as principal components regression, the number of score vectors can be treated as a tuning parameter and selected by cross-validation. This provides a criterion linked to predictive performance. A component set that summarizes variance well is not necessarily the set that best predicts a particular outcome. The goal of the analysis therefore determines how component count should be selected.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast retaining components for visualization with retaining them for a regression model."
Key point:
- Exploratory component selection is often subjective.
- In supervised use, component count can be tuned by cross-validation.
18. Q: How can PCA be used before regression, classification, or clustering?
A: Show answer
PCA can transform a large feature matrix into a smaller matrix containing the first M score vectors. These scores can then serve as inputs to regression, classification, or clustering. If meaningful signal is concentrated in leading components, reducing dimensions can remove some noise and make later analyses more manageable. However, discarded components may contain task-relevant information. The reduction should be judged against the downstream objective, not only by the variance retained. The chapter also illustrates clustering on PCA scores as a possible denoising step.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a pipeline from raw features through PCA scores to a downstream model and state what may be discarded."
Key point:
- PCA scores provide a reduced feature representation.
- Dimension reduction may reduce noise but can also discard relevant information.
19. Q: What is clustering, and what does it mean for observations to belong to the same cluster?
A: Show answer
Clustering seeks subgroups in a dataset. Observations in the same group should be similar to one another, while observations in different groups should be more dissimilar. This requires a definition of similarity that depends on the data and the scientific question. Patient samples may be grouped by measurements to explore disease subtypes; shoppers may be grouped by purchase patterns. Cluster labels are algorithm outputs, not known response values. A clustering result is a proposed organization of the data, not proof that the population contains fixed natural categories.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a clustering application and define what similarity should mean before selecting an algorithm."
Key point:
- Clustering seeks homogeneous groups within a dataset.
- Similarity is central and often domain-specific.
20. Q: How does PCA differ from clustering?
A: Show answer
PCA finds a low-dimensional representation that captures a large fraction of variation. Clustering finds homogeneous subgroups. PCA produces continuous component coordinates, while clustering assigns group membership. A PCA plot may reveal visual groupings, but PCA itself does not assign cluster labels. Clustering can group observations without producing a low-dimensional coordinate system. The methods can complement one another: PCA can help visualize data or provide reduced features for clustering. A direction of high variance does not necessarily separate natural groups, and groups may exist in directions with relatively little overall variance.
Study instruction:
- "State what PCA and clustering each return for the same dataset and explain how their results might be combined."
Key point:
- PCA summarizes variation; clustering identifies subgroups.
- A PCA plot is not itself a clustering solution.
21. Q: What is K-means clustering, and what must be specified before it is run?
A: Show answer
K-means partitions observations into K distinct, non-overlapping clusters. Each observation belongs to exactly one cluster, and the method seeks to minimize total within-cluster variation. The analyst must specify K in advance. Each cluster is represented by a centroid, the vector of feature means for observations assigned to that group. K-means is convenient when a plausible group count can be explored, but an inappropriate K may split one group or merge distinct groups. Cluster labels are arbitrary identifiers, not an ordered scale.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what K controls and why cluster labels do not imply ranking."
Key point:
- K-means requires the number of clusters in advance.
- Each observation is assigned to exactly one cluster.
22. Q: What objective does K-means minimize?
A: Show answer
K-means minimizes total within-cluster variation across the K groups. The chapter's common measure uses squared Euclidean distances among observations within each cluster, normalized by cluster size. This favors groups whose members are close in the chosen feature space. The result depends on feature selection and scale. Exact optimization is difficult because the number of possible partitions grows rapidly with n and K, so the practical algorithm seeks a local optimum rather than guaranteeing the global minimum.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how squared Euclidean distances contribute to within-cluster variation."
Key point:
- K-means minimizes total within-cluster variation.
- The objective depends on features, scaling, and distance geometry.
23. Q: What are the main steps of the K-means algorithm?
A: Show answer
K-means begins by randomly assigning observations to K groups. It then alternates between calculating each cluster centroid as the feature-wise mean and assigning each observation to the nearest centroid using Euclidean distance. The steps repeat until assignments stop changing. Each iteration does not increase the objective, so the procedure converges to a local optimum. The method is intuitive and practical, but the result can depend on the initial random assignment and need not be the global best partition.
Study instruction:
- "Perform one manual iteration on a small two-feature dataset and repeat until assignments stabilize."
Key point:
- K-means alternates centroid calculation and nearest-centroid assignment.
- It stops when cluster assignments no longer change.
24. Q: Why can K-means produce different results on repeated runs?
A: Show answer
K-means uses random initial assignments, and its optimization can converge to different local optima depending on the starting configuration. The chapter illustrates repeated runs with K equal to three that produce different objective values. A better candidate has the smaller total within-cluster sum of squares. Analysts should therefore run the algorithm from multiple starts and retain the best objective rather than relying on one random initialization.
Study instruction:
- "Run K-means from several starts and compare total within-cluster sum of squares and assignments."
Key point:
- Random initialization can lead to different local optima.
- Multiple starts reduce the risk of accepting a poor local solution.
25. Q: How can K-means be made more reproducible and less dependent on initialization?
A: Show answer
Use multiple random starts, such as 20 or 50, and select the result with the smallest within-cluster objective. In R, nstart controls the number of initial configurations tried, and set.seed fixes the random-number sequence so the starts can be reproduced. These practices reduce the chance of a poor local optimum and improve reproducibility, but do not prove global optimality or scientific validity. A reproducible result can still use an unsuitable K, scale, or feature set.
Study instruction:
- "Compare nstart equal to one with a larger value, set a random seed, and record the objective."
Key point:
- Use multiple starts and retain the lowest within-cluster objective.
- Set a random seed when reproducibility is required.
26. Q: Why is choosing K difficult?
A: Show answer
If K is too small, distinct subgroups may be merged; if it is too large, a coherent group may be split into smaller groups. The chapter gives no universal rule for selecting K. The choice should be explored through domain knowledge, interpretability, the within-cluster objective, and whether the result answers the practical question. Its simulation shows that increasing K can split an existing group rather than reveal a genuinely new population structure. K is therefore an analytical assumption that needs justification.
Study instruction:
- "Fit several plausible K values and compare the resulting groups and their usefulness."
Key point:
- K is an input assumption, not an automatic discovery by standard K-means.
- More clusters do not necessarily produce more meaningful segmentation.
27. Q: What is hierarchical clustering, and how does it differ from K-means?
A: Show answer
Agglomerative hierarchical clustering builds a nested sequence of groups represented by a dendrogram. Each observation begins as its own cluster, and the algorithm repeatedly merges the two most similar current clusters until all observations are joined. Unlike K-means, the analyst does not need to specify the final cluster count before constructing the hierarchy. A dendrogram can later be cut at different heights to obtain different numbers of groups. The result depends on the distance measure and linkage rule.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast the inputs and outputs of K-means and agglomerative hierarchical clustering."
Key point:
- Hierarchical clustering builds a nested tree of merges.
- A dendrogram supports several possible cluster counts after fitting.
28. Q: How should the vertical height of a dendrogram be interpreted?
A: Show answer
Each leaf represents an observation, and each fusion joins observations or previously formed clusters. The vertical height records the dissimilarity between the groups being joined under the selected linkage rule. Low fusions indicate relatively similar groups; high fusions indicate more dissimilar groups. Height is not time or cluster importance. It records the level at which the algorithm merged branches and provides a basis for interpreting structure and choosing a cut.
Study instruction:
- "Trace two leaves to their first common fusion and explain what its height means."
Key point:
- Fusion height represents inter-cluster dissimilarity.
- The vertical axis carries similarity information, not horizontal spacing.
29. Q: Why is horizontal proximity between dendrogram leaves not a reliable similarity measure?
A: Show answer
At every fusion, the two branches can be swapped left-to-right without changing the meaning of the dendrogram. Many horizontal arrangements therefore represent the same hierarchy. Two observations that appear adjacent are not necessarily more similar than observations farther apart horizontally. Similarity should be inferred from the height at which their branches first join. The chapter's nine-observation example demonstrates that horizontal placement can be misleading.
Study instruction:
- "Find horizontally adjacent leaves and compare their first common fusion height with other observations."
Key point:
- Horizontal leaf order is arbitrary.
- Use the height of the first common fusion to interpret similarity.
30. Q: How does cutting a dendrogram produce a chosen number of clusters?
A: Show answer
A horizontal cut separates the branches beneath it into distinct groups. A cut high in the tree produces fewer clusters because more branches have already merged; a lower cut produces more clusters. One dendrogram can therefore yield two, three, or other numbers of clusters without rerunning the hierarchy. The cut should be justified by the fusion structure and the application, because a visually obvious height is not always available.
Study instruction:
- "Draw cuts at two heights and count the separate branches below each cut."
Key point:
- Cut height controls the number of clusters extracted.
- One hierarchy can support several possible cluster counts.
31. Q: What does it mean that hierarchical clusters are nested?
A: Show answer
Groups obtained from a lower cut are contained within groups obtained from a higher cut. As the cut moves upward, groups merge; previously merged observations do not split into separate branches. This is convenient because one tree displays a family of clusterings, but it assumes that finer groupings can be obtained by subdividing coarser ones. The chapter gives an example where the best two-group split might be by gender while the best three-group split might be by nationality. Such partitions are not nested and cannot both be represented optimally by one hierarchy.
Study instruction:
- "Construct two non-nested partitions and explain why one dendrogram cannot represent both as optimal."
Key point:
- Hierarchical solutions are nested by construction.
- Real subgroup structures do not always follow a nested hierarchy.
32. Q: What is the agglomerative hierarchical clustering procedure?
A: Show answer
The algorithm begins with n observations as n separate clusters and a dissimilarity measure for every pair. It finds the two clusters that are least dissimilar, merges them, recalculates dissimilarities among the remaining groups, and repeats until all observations are joined. Fusion heights are recorded in the dendrogram. Linkage is needed to define dissimilarity when one or both groups contain multiple observations. Both the original distance and the linkage rule affect the final hierarchy and should be reported.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the first three merges of a small dataset and identify when inter-cluster distances are recalculated."
Key point:
- Agglomerative clustering repeatedly merges the most similar current groups.
- Linkage defines dissimilarity between groups.
33. Q: How do complete, single, average, and centroid linkage differ?
A: Show answer
Complete linkage uses the largest pairwise distance between members of two clusters. Single linkage uses the smallest. Average linkage uses the mean of all pairwise distances. Centroid linkage uses the distance between the two cluster mean vectors. These definitions encode different notions of group similarity and can produce different dendrograms from the same data. The chapter generally prefers complete and average over single because they tend to produce more balanced groups. Centroid linkage can produce inversions.
Study instruction:
- "For two small clusters, identify the maximum, minimum, average, and centroid-to-centroid distance."
Key point:
- Linkage specifies how group-to-group dissimilarity is measured.
- Different linkage rules can produce different hierarchies.
34. Q: Why can single linkage produce long trailing clusters?
A: Show answer
Single linkage merges groups when their closest pair of observations is close, even if other members are far apart. This can create a chain effect: individual observations or small groups attach one by one to an extended cluster. The NCI60 example shows that single linkage tends to produce trailing clusters, while complete and average linkage tend to produce more balanced groups. Single linkage is not automatically wrong; it represents a particular definition of similarity. Its suitability depends on whether chain-like connectivity is meaningful for the application.
Study instruction:
- "Use a chain of points to show how nearest-neighbor connections can join observations that are far apart overall."
Key point:
- Single linkage is governed by the closest pair between clusters.
- It can create extended clusters that absorb observations one by one.
35. Q: What is an inversion in centroid linkage?
A: Show answer
Centroid linkage measures distance between cluster centroids. It can produce an inversion, where two clusters are fused at a height lower than the height at which one or both component clusters were previously formed. This violates the intuitive expectation of non-decreasing merge heights and makes the dendrogram harder to visualize and interpret. The chapter notes that centroid linkage is used in some fields, including genomics, but its inversion problem is a major drawback. Analysts should inspect the tree and understand the linkage rather than assume every dendrogram has monotonic heights.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a centroid distance can create a merge below an earlier fusion and why that complicates interpretation."
Key point:
- Centroid linkage can produce non-monotonic fusion heights.
- Inversions undermine the intuitive reading of dendrogram levels.
36. Q: How should an analyst choose between Euclidean and correlation-based distance?
A: Show answer
Euclidean distance measures absolute differences in feature values. Correlation-based distance focuses on similarity in observation profiles, so two observations can be close if their features move together even when their absolute levels differ. In the retail example, Euclidean distance may group infrequent shoppers because they buy fewer items, while correlation distance may group shoppers with similar preferences despite different purchase volumes. The correct choice depends on what similarity means for the question.
Study instruction:
- "Describe two observations with similar profiles but different magnitudes and compare the distances."
Key point:
- Euclidean distance emphasizes magnitude differences.
- Correlation distance emphasizes similarity in profile shape.
37. Q: Why does variable scaling matter when computing clustering distances?
A: Show answer
Distance-based clustering can be dominated by variables with large numerical ranges or variances. If features use different units, changing units can change distances and therefore the groups. In the shopping example, frequent purchases such as socks may dominate distances relative to rare purchases such as computers. Standardizing features gives variables more comparable scales but also changes their effective weights. The same issue applies to K-means and hierarchical clustering. Scaling should be justified by the meaning of each variable, not applied mechanically.
Study instruction:
- "Compare cluster assignments before and after standardization and identify which variables dominate each distance calculation."
Key point:
- Distance-based clustering is sensitive to feature scale.
- Standardization changes the effective importance of variables.
38. Q: What practical decisions can materially change a clustering result?
A: Show answer
Important decisions include whether to center or standardize variables, which dissimilarity measure to use, which linkage to choose, where to cut a dendrogram, and what K to use for K-means. Each can materially change the groups. Analysts should compare reasonable alternatives and examine which patterns remain interpretable. There is no single universally correct solution for every dataset. The goal is not to find a visually attractive arrangement in isolation, but to understand how the result depends on assumptions and whether it informs the scientific or operational question.
Study instruction:
- "Create a sensitivity table for scaling, distance, linkage, cut height, and K and record their effects."
Key point:
- Clustering outcomes depend strongly on modeling choices.
- Compare plausible alternatives rather than treating one solution as definitive.
39. Q: How can the validity and stability of clusters be investigated?
A: Show answer
A central question is whether groups represent real structure or patterns created by noise. One test is whether an independent sample shows similar groupings. The chapter notes that methods exist for assigning evidence measures to clusters, but no single best method is universally accepted. It also recommends clustering subsets of the data to see whether patterns recur. A result that changes sharply when a modest subset is removed may be unstable. Validation should combine quantitative checks, domain knowledge, independent evidence where possible, and transparent sensitivity analysis.
Study instruction:
- "Refit clustering on several random subsets and compare whether broad groups recur."
Key point:
- Clusters should be tested for stability and evidence beyond the original fit.
- There is no single universal validation method for all clustering tasks.
40. Q: Why can outliers distort K-means and hierarchical clustering?
A: Show answer
Both methods assign every observation to a cluster. If some observations are extreme and do not naturally belong to the main groups, forcing them into a cluster can distort the structure. In K-means, unusual points can influence centroids; in hierarchical clustering, they may form separate branches or affect late merges. The chapter mentions mixture models as a soft alternative for accommodating outliers. Analysts should investigate unusual cases and determine whether they are valid observations, measurement problems, or members of a different process before treating their assignments as meaningful.
Study instruction:
- "Add an extreme observation to a small dataset and compare changes in centroids or the dendrogram."
Key point:
- Standard K-means and hierarchical clustering force all observations into groups.
- Outliers can distort clusters and may need separate treatment.
41. Q: Why should clustering results be interpreted as hypotheses rather than absolute truth?
A: Show answer
Clustering depends on preprocessing, distance, linkage, initialization, and cluster count, and it can be unstable under data perturbations. The chapter recommends examining patterns across reasonable choices and clustering subsets to see what consistently emerges. Results should be a starting point for scientific hypotheses and further study, preferably with independent data. This caution matters when cluster labels might influence medical, commercial, or organizational decisions. A cluster is an output of a particular method applied to particular measurements, not proof of a permanent identity or natural category.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite an overconfident cluster conclusion as a hypothesis and state what independent evidence is needed."
Key point:
- Clusters are exploratory findings, not unquestionable facts.
- Report assumptions, sensitivity, and the need for further validation.
42. Q: How is PCA performed in R using prcomp, and what are its main outputs?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses prcomp on USArrests with scale set to TRUE. The function centers variables by default and can scale them to unit standard deviation. The center and scale outputs record preprocessing values, rotation contains loadings, x contains observation scores, and sdev contains component standard deviations. The biplot function can display scores and loadings together. These outputs make the transformation reproducible and show how the original features are mapped into component coordinates. The scaling choice must be recorded because it changes the resulting components.
Study instruction:
- "Run prcomp with and without scaling and identify center, scale, rotation, x, and sdev."
Key point:
- prcomp returns both component directions and observation scores.
- Centering and scaling settings must be documented.
43. Q: How can PVE be calculated from prcomp output?
A: Show answer
The sdev output contains the standard deviation of each component. Squaring these values gives component variances. Dividing each variance by the sum of all component variances gives PVE, and a cumulative sum gives cumulative PVE. In USArrests, PC1 explains about 62 percent and PC2 about 24.7 percent. The same information can be displayed in individual and cumulative plots. Use the same centered and scaled data for all calculations; mixing preprocessing conventions leads to inconsistent comparisons.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate PVE from squared sdev values and verify that individual proportions sum to one."
Key point:
- Square component standard deviations to obtain component variances.
- Divide by total variance to calculate PVE.
44. Q: How are K-means and hierarchical clustering implemented in the R labs?
A: Show answer
The K-means lab uses kmeans with a chosen K and a larger nstart to try multiple initial assignments. The output includes labels, centers, sizes, and within-cluster sums of squares. The hierarchical lab uses dist to compute Euclidean distances, hclust to build a dendrogram, and cutree to extract labels at a chosen cut. The scale function standardizes variables before distances, while as.dist converts a symmetric correlation-based dissimilarity matrix for hclust. Each function performs a distinct stage and its settings should be recorded.
Study instruction:
- "Recreate one K-means and one hierarchical fit and identify the function used for each stage."
Key point:
- kmeans, dist, hclust, cutree, scale, and as.dist have distinct roles.
- Report the settings that determine the resulting groups.
45. Q: What does the NCI60 example illustrate about PCA in high-dimensional data?
A: Show answer
The NCI60 dataset contains 64 cancer cell lines and 6,830 gene-expression measurements per line. Plotting all pairwise feature relationships is impractical, so PCA provides a low-dimensional view. The chapter scales genes to unit standard deviation for its illustration, while noting that not scaling could also be argued. The first few score plots show that cell lines of the same cancer type tend to lie near one another, although separation is imperfect. PCA can therefore expose broad structure in data that cannot be visualized directly in the original feature space.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the dimensions of NCI60 and why PCA is useful for visualizing thousands of features."
Key point:
- PCA makes high-dimensional gene-expression patterns easier to inspect.
- Similar cancer types tend to be near one another in leading component space, but not perfectly separated.
46. Q: How should the NCI60 PCA variance results be interpreted?
A: Show answer
In the NCI60 analysis, PC1 explains about 11.4 percent of variance, PC2 about 6.8 percent, and the first five together about 31.9 percent. The first seven explain roughly 40 percent, with a visible decline in additional variance after about the seventh component. This is less concentrated than USArrests, where the first two components explain nearly 87 percent. The number of components needed for a useful summary depends on the dataset. A modest cumulative PVE can still reveal interpretable structure in a high-dimensional application.
Study instruction:
- "Compare PVE concentration in USArrests and NCI60 and explain why two components summarize one more completely."
Key point:
- Variance can be spread across many components in high-dimensional data.
- Component count should reflect the data and analysis purpose.
47. Q: What did hierarchical clustering reveal in the NCI60 example?
A: Show answer
The chapter clusters 64 cell lines using gene-expression measurements after standardizing genes, comparing complete, average, and single linkage with Euclidean distance. Linkage changes the dendrogram: complete and average produce more balanced groups, while single creates trailing clusters. Cutting complete linkage into four groups places all leukemia lines in one cluster, but breast cancer lines are spread across several. The groups therefore show some correspondence with known cancer types without reproducing them perfectly. Cancer labels are used after clustering to interpret the result, not as clustering inputs.
Study instruction:
- "Explain which data formed the clusters and how known cancer labels were used afterward."
Key point:
- Unsupervised clustering can reveal partial alignment with known biological categories.
- Linkage choice changes the resulting hierarchy.
48. Q: Why can K-means and hierarchical clustering produce different groups from the same data?
A: Show answer
K-means iteratively assigns observations to centroids to reduce within-cluster variation for a prespecified K. Hierarchical clustering constructs a sequence of merges determined by pairwise distances and linkage, then a cut is selected. Even when both return four groups, assignments need not agree. The NCI60 example shows some matching clusters and other substantial differences. The methods are alternative views of the data, not interchangeable procedures. Agreement can be informative, while disagreement should prompt investigation of the assumptions and structure each method emphasizes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare cross-tabulations from both methods at the same K and identify observations assigned differently."
Key point:
- The methods use different grouping mechanisms.
- The same cluster count does not guarantee matching assignments.
49. Q: How can PCA scores be used to simplify clustering?
A: Show answer
Instead of clustering the full feature matrix, an analyst can cluster the scores for the first few components. This reduces dimensions and may suppress noise if signal is concentrated in leading components. The NCI60 example clusters the first five scores and obtains results different from clustering the full data. PCA may act as denoising, and K-means can also be applied to score vectors. This is not guaranteed to improve results: discarded components may contain useful group information. Compare the reduced and full-feature analyses in context.
Study instruction:
- "Cluster original features and selected PCA scores, then explain how the representation changes distances and assignments."
Key point:
- PCA scores provide lower-dimensional inputs for clustering.
- Dimension reduction may denoise data but can discard relevant structure.
50. Q: What should be considered when a leading component appears to capture a technical batch effect?
A: Show answer
A leading component may reflect machine, processing day, or another technical condition rather than the scientific effect of interest. The chapter's exercise describes gene-expression samples processed over time on different machines, with PC1 showing a trend related to processing order. Simply subtracting PC1 before testing treatment differences is risky because it may contain both technical variation and genuine treatment signal, especially if batch and treatment are confounded. Examine metadata and how treatment and machine are distributed before adjusting. Do not remove a component mechanically without understanding the study design.
Study instruction:
- "Map processing order, machine, and treatment labels; assess confounding before proposing adjustment."
Key point:
- A high-variance component may reflect technical structure rather than scientific signal.
- Do not remove a component mechanically without investigating confounding.
51. Q: What are the most important lessons from Unsupervised Learning?
A: Show answer
PCA constructs ordered linear combinations that summarize variance, support visualization, and provide reduced features; results depend on centering, scaling, and component count. Clustering seeks homogeneous groups: K-means minimizes within-cluster variation for a chosen K, while hierarchical clustering builds a dendrogram through repeated merges. Both depend on scale, distance, linkage, initialization, and cluster count. Their results can be unstable and difficult to validate because no response variable provides a direct correctness test. The examples show useful structure in crime, retail, and gene-expression data, but findings should be treated as hypotheses for further investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter under PCA, K-means, hierarchical clustering, and validation, noting key assumptions and limitations."
Key point:
- PCA summarizes variation; clustering searches for subgroups.
- Unsupervised findings require sensitivity analysis, domain interpretation, and cautious reporting.
